Within the ſacred Temple made this Prayer: 
Great Deity! Who in thy Hands do'ſt bear 
That Iron Scepter which poor Mortals fear; 
Who wanting Eyes thy ſelf, reſpecteſt none, 
And neither ſpar'ſt the Laurel, nor the Crown! 
O thou, whom all Mankind in vain withſtand, 
Each of hoſe Blood maſt one Day ſtain thy Hand! 
O thou, who ev'ry Eye that ſees the Light, 
Cloſeſt for ever in the Shades of Night!

HELEN to PARIS. 
I Was it for this your Fate did Shelter find 
F rom ſ ſrelling Seas, and ev'ry faithleſs Wind ? 
55 tho' a diſtant Country brought you forth, 
\ Your Uſage here was equal to your Worth.) 
1 Does this deſerve to be rewarded ſo? 
Did you come ct a Stranger, or a Foe » | 
Your partial Judgment may Perhaps complain, 
And think me barb'rous for n my juſt Diſdain; 
Ill bred then let me be, but not unchaſte, 
Nor my clear Fame with any Spot defac'd 
Tho' in my Face there's no affected Frown, 


Nor in my Carriage a feign'd Niceneſs ſhown, 
„ keep my Honour ſtill without a Stain, 

Nor has my Love made any Coxcomb vain. 
Your Boldneſs I with Admiration ſee; q 6 
What Hope had you to gain a Queen like me: 2 


f Becauſe a Hero forc'd me once away, 


Am! thought fit to be a ſecond Prey? 
Had I been won, I had deſerv'd your Blame, 


But ſure my Part was nothing but the Shame:

O where doſt thou retire?” and why doſt thou return 

Sometimes with pow*rful Charms to hurry me away, 

From Pleaſures of the Night, and Bus neſs of the Day? 

Ev'n now too far tranſported, I am fain 

To check thy Courſe, and uſe the needful Rein. 

As all is Dullneſs, when the Fancy's bad ; 

So, without Judgment, Fancy is but mad: 

And Judgment has a boundleſs Influence 

Not only in the choice of Words, or Senſe, 

But on the World, on Manners, and on Men; 

Fancy is but the Feather of the Pen; 

Reaſon is that ſubſtantial, uſeful part, 

Which gains the Head, while t'other wins the Heart. 
Here I ſhould all the various ſorts of Verſe, 

And the whole Art of Poetry rehearſe;

Is above his, who writes a borrow'd Part. 
Yet modern Laws are made for later Faults, 
And new Abſurdities inſpire new Thoughts; 
What need has Satyr then to live on Theft, 
When ſo much freſh occaſion ſtill is left? 
Fertile our Soil, and full of rankeſt Weeds, 
And Monſters worſe than ever Nilus breeds. 
But hold, the Fools ſhall have no cauſe to fear; 
Tis Wit and Senſe that is the Subject here: 
Defects of witty Men deſerve a Cure, 
And thoſe who are ſo, will ev'n this endure.

An ESSAT on POETRY 


But he, diſcloſing ſacred Myſteries, 

Has ſhewn where all the mighty Magick lies; | 
Deſcrib'd the Seeds, and in what order ſown, 

That have to ſuch a vaſt Proportion grown. 


Sure, from ſome Angel he the Secret knew, 
Who through this Labyrinth has lent the Clue! 
But what, alas, avails it poor Mankind, 
To ſee this promis'd Land, yet ſtay behind? 
The Way is ſhewn, but who has Strength to go? 
Who can all Sciences profoundly know? 
Whoſe Fancy flies beyond weak Reaſon's Sight, 
And yet has Judgment to direct it right? | 
Whoſe juſt Diſcernment, Virgil- like, is ſuch, 
Never to ſay too little, or too much? 
Let ſuch a Man begin without delay; 
But he muſt do beyond what I can ſay; 
Muſt above Taſſo's lofty Flights prevail, 
Succeed where Spencer, and ev'n Milton fail.

gurative Expreſſion, yet much invalidates a Narration of ſuch a 
vaſt Importance, that Tropes and Figures ſeem a little improper

4 Reaſon.) Leſt their aforeſaid Argument in defence of this

ference ſeems as great ſometimes between one Man and another, 
and (perhaps they may ſay merrily) even between themſelves 
and their Adverſaries. Secondly, they find this Reaſon to be 
ſuch a narrow, miſleading, uncertain Faculty, that in their Opi- 
nion *tis much unworthy of being the great Credential from the 
moſt high God for domincering over all our Fellow Creatures, 
who indeed ſeem neither to owe us, nor pay us Obedience.

Too fond of Fame, our Poet ſoars too high; q 
Yet freely owns he wants the Wings to flyt- 
So ſenſible of his preſumptuous Thought, = 
That he confeſſes while he does the Fault: 
This to the Fair will no great wonder prove, 
ho oft in Bluſhes yield to what they love. 


Of greateſt Aclions, and of nobleft Men, 
This Story moſt deſerves a Poet's Pen. Y 
For who can wiſh a Scene more juftly fam d, 
hen Rome and mighty Julius are but nam d? 
That State of Heroes, who the World had brad d! 
That wondrous Man, who ſuch a State inflav'd! 
Tet loth he was to take ſo rough a way, 

And after govern'd with fo mild a Sway, 

At diftance now of ſev'nteen hundred Years, 
Methinks a lovely Raviſher appears; 

Yr hom, tho forbid hy Virtue to excuſe, 

A Nymph might pardon, and could ſcarce refuſe.

That melancholic Caſſius needs obſerving; WL! 

If cer I could be capable of Fear, 

I think it would be of that penſive Caſſius. 

He loves not Learning, no not Poetry; 

Nor is his ſullen humour pleasd with Muſick. 

When others laugh, he ſo demurely ſmiles 

As if he thought it Meanneſs to be merry. 

Seldom he likes what others moſt approve, 

And loves to praiſe what all men elſe diſlike. 

Such Men as he are never at their Eaſe, 

While they behold a greater than themſelves, 

Yet he is brave, and ſhall have due Preferment. 


Exit Junius.

Friends, Countrymen, and Romans, hear me gently 7 ; 
| come to bury C#ſar; not to praiſe” him. 

Lo here the fatal end of all his Glory: 
The Evil that Men do lives after them; 
The Good is often bury'd in their Graves; 
So let it be with Ceſar. Noble Brutus 
Has told you Ceſar was ambitious: 


If he was ſo, then he was much to blame J 
And he has dearly paid for his Offence. 

Here, under leave of Brutus and the reſt, 

I come to do my duty to dead Czfar. 

He was my Friend, faithful and juſt to me ; 
But yet it ſeems he was Ambitious. 

Brutus has ſaid it, J muſt fay it too; 

For Brutus is a Man of ſtricteſt Honour. 
Thouſands of Captives Ceſar brought to Rome, 
Whoſe Ranſom often fill'd the Publick Coffers.
